   563→    <div class="blade__description">
   564→        
   565→    <p>If the past few years have taught supply chain leaders anything, it’s that the old playbook doesn’t hold up under pressure. Demand can shift overnight. A weather event stalls shipments. New tariffs or sudden spikes in fuel costs can quickly change the economics of moving goods. Even carefully built plans can’t always adapt fast enough.</p>
   566→<p>An AI supply chain changes how those moments are handled. Instead of reacting after something breaks, artificial intelligence continuously analyzes patterns across forecasting, inventory, logistics, and supplier data to anticipate issues before they escalate. You can make faster and more confident decisions because you’re operating with live insight into how your network is performing. Plus, AI agents reduce manual work and increase accuracy to ultimately keep the supply chain moving faster.</p>
   567→<p>This guide explains what AI in supply chain settings means and how to implement it without overcomplicating your technology stack.</p>
   568→
   569→
   570→    </div>
   571→    
   572→
   573→        </article>
   574→    </div>
   575→
   576→    
   577→</section>

   629→
   630→<h2 class="headline text__headline--large" style=" ">
   631→    
   632→        Key takeaways
   633→    
   634→
   635→</h2>
   636→
   637→        
   638→    
   639→</header>
   640→    
   641→    <div class="blade__description">
   642→        
   643→    <ul>
   644→<li>AI supply chain solutions apply predictive analytics, automation, and decision intelligence to improve operational performance across the network.</li>
   645→<li>AI in supply chain strengthens demand forecasting, inventory planning, and logistics optimization through continuous learning models.</li>
   646→<li>Digital twins and predictive risk modeling help organizations prepare for disruptions before they cascade.</li>
   647→<li>End-to-end visibility across procurement, manufacturing, logistics, and distribution supports faster, more informed decisions.</li>
   648→<li>Salesforce introduces AI agents into supply chain workflows to increase speed, accuracy, and automation.</li>
   649→</ul>

   702→    <div class="container container--min-spacing-bottom container--min-spacing-top">
   703→        <article class="grid grid_main text__blade--link_below text--left
   704→        "
   705→                 data-module-id="1715380369670_b22" data-module-name="What is AI supply chain?" data-module-position="1" data-module-type="headline" data-module-track-impressions="">
   706→            
   707→<header class="textmainbody__blade-heading grid grid_sub">
   708→    
   709→        
   710→            
   711→
   712→<h2 class="headline text__headline--large" style=" ">
   713→    
   714→        What is AI supply chain?
   715→    
   716→
   717→</h2>
   718→
   719→        
   720→    
   721→</header>
   722→    
   723→    <div class="blade__description">
   724→        
   725→    <p>AI supply chain refers to the use of artificial intelligence to improve how goods move from supplier to customer. It applies machine learning and predictive models to areas like forecasting, inventory planning, transportation, and risk management so operations can adjust before problems spread.</p>
   726→<p>It’s not just about making adjustments, either. AI in supply chain also means accelerating any process, reducing human middleware (which is expensive, inefficient, and error-prone), and making updates to processes quickly.</p>
   727→<p>Traditional supply chains operate in cycles. Teams plan, execute, review, and then course correct. Supply chain AI changes that process. You don’t have to wait for reports to highlight what went wrong since the system continuously analyzes signals across procurement, production, and distribution to anticipate what’s coming next.</p>
   728→<p>AI in supply chain environments connects data that historically lived in separate systems and translates it into guided actions. If a supplier delay will create a stockout next week, you see it early enough to adjust replenishment or reroute inventory. If demand is accelerating in one region, you can shift distribution before shelves run empty. </p>
   729→
   730→
   731→    </div>
   732→    
   733→
   734→        </article>
   735→    </div>
   736→
   737→    
   738→</section>
   739→
   740→</div><div data-blade-id="1717796473733_12ue" data-blade-position="5" data-blade-name="sf/textmainbody" data-blade-variant="textmainbody" data-blade-source="www" data-blade-type="blade">

   782→        <article class="grid grid_main text__blade--link_below text--left
   783→        "
   784→                 data-module-id="1717796473733_12ue" data-module-name="Why AI in supply chain is becoming essential" data-module-position="1" data-module-type="headline" data-module-track-impressions="">
   785→            
   786→<header class="textmainbody__blade-heading grid grid_sub">
   787→    
   788→        
   789→            
   790→
   791→<h2 class="headline text__headline--large" style=" ">
   792→    
   793→        Why AI in supply chain is becoming essential
   794→    
   795→
   796→</h2>
   797→
   798→        
   799→    
   800→</header>
   801→    
   802→    <div class="blade__description">
   803→        
   804→    <p>Supply chains aren’t operating in stable conditions anymore. Global volatility, shifting trade policies, labor shortages, and unpredictable demand have made long planning cycles risky. Margins are tighter, and yet customer expectations are higher, which means even small delays can cause far-reaching ripples.</p>
   805→<p>At the same time, data volumes have exploded. Every order, shipment, production run, and customer interaction generates signals. Without AI, most of that information sits in systems until someone pulls a report.</p>
   806→<p>AI in supply chain closes that gap. It helps organizations respond to demand swings, supplier instability, and transportation delays with speed and precision. What once required manual analysis across multiple teams can now happen continuously, with clear recommendations on where to adjust. That shift is why supply chain AI is becoming more and more essential.</p>
   807→
   808→
   809→    </div>
   810→    
   811→
   812→        </article>
   813→    </div>
   814→
   815→    
   816→</section>

   818→</div><div data-blade-id="1719930640697_o6s" data-blade-position="6" data-blade-name="sf/textmainbody" data-blade-variant="textmainbody" data-blade-source="www" data-blade-type="blade">

   860→        <article class="grid grid_main text__blade--link_below text--left

   864→<header class="textmainbody__blade-heading grid grid_sub">
   865→    
   866→        
   867→            
   868→
   869→<h2 class="headline text__headline--large" style=" ">
   870→    
   871→        Demand forecasting with predictive AI
   872→    
   873→
   874→</h2>
   875→
   876→        
   877→    
   878→</header>
   879→    
   880→    <div class="blade__description">
   881→        
   882→    <p>Forecasting has always been a blend of intuition and numbers. Teams dive into historical sales data, keep an eye on promotional schedules, and make informed guesses about what customers will want next quarter. However, when conditions change mid-cycle, those static forecasts can fall short.</p>
   883→<p><a href="https://www.salesforce.com/artificial-intelligence/predictive-ai/" target="_self">Predictive AI</a> comes into play here. Advanced supply chain systems enhance demand forecasting by analyzing vast amounts of data more frequently. Instead of just looking at past sales, these predictive models consider current order trends, regional demand signals, and other factors that affect buying behavior. This means they can spot emerging trends earlier and adjust predictions before inventory issues arise.</p>
   884→<p>Note that this doesn’t eliminate human judgment. Planners move from building forecasts manually to supervising models that continuously refine projections as new data comes in.</p>
   885→
   886→
   887→    </div>
   888→

   947→<h3 class="headline text__headline--medium" style=" ">
   948→    
   949→        From static forecasting to continuous learning
   950→    
   951→
   952→</h3>
   953→
   954→        
   955→    
   956→</header>
   957→    
   958→    <div class="blade__description">
   959→        
   960→    <p>Traditional forecasts are often updated on a monthly or quarterly basis. In contrast, AI-driven demand sensing operates in shorter cycles. When customer behavior shifts, the models adapt quickly. This allows for real-time assessment of promotional impacts and immediate re-forecasting in response to sudden demand changes.</p>
   961→<p>Over time, this strategy leads to more stable inventory levels and minimizes unexpected challenges.</p>

  1033→</header>

  1035→    <div class="blade__description">
  1036→        
  1037→    <p>Transportation decisions rarely stay isolated. One delay can ripple across delivery windows, labor schedules, and customer commitments.</p>
  1038→<p>AI supply chain systems continuously evaluate how goods move through the network. Shipment volumes, carrier reliability, and current route conditions feed into models that highlight better paths when adjustments make sense. If a fulfillment center starts to slow down, inventory can be redirected before orders fall behind. If delivery performance drops in a region, planners can see the trend early enough to shift capacity.</p>

  1113→        
  1114→    <p>AI evaluates distribution centers, cross-dock facilities, and last-mile carriers as part of one connected system. When performance starts to drift in one area, planners can see it early and adjust volume before it spreads.</p>
  1115→<p>Over time, routing decisions stay closer to real operating conditions. That stability shows up in delivery performance and cost control.</p>

  1177→
  1178→<h2 class="headline text__headline--large" style=" ">
  1179→    
  1180→        Inventory optimization and warehouse automation
  1181→    
  1182→
  1183→</h2>
  1184→
  1185→        
  1186→    
  1187→</header>
  1188→    
  1189→    <div class="blade__description">
  1190→        
  1191→    <p>Inventory decisions sit at the center of supply chain performance. Order too much, and cash gets tied up in excess stock. Order too little, and service levels slip.</p>
  1192→<p>AI supply chain systems monitor sales velocity, replenishment cycles, and supplier lead times to keep inventory aligned with actual demand. Planners can adjust purchase orders or redistribute stock before imbalances grow. Safety stock levels become more responsive because they reflect current conditions, not assumptions made months earlier.</p>
  1193→<p>Warehouse automation follows the same principle. AI supports replenishment triggers and task prioritization so inventory moves where it’s needed most.</p>

  1255→
  1256→<h3 class="headline text__headline--medium" style=" ">
  1257→    
  1258→        Intelligent warehouse operations
  1259→    
  1260→
  1261→</h3>
  1262→
  1263→        
  1264→    
  1265→</header>
  1266→    
  1267→    <div class="blade__description">
  1268→        
  1269→    <p>In the warehouse, AI can prioritize work based on what’s shipping next and where items sit on the floor. That reduces wasted travel and helps supervisors spot bottlenecks sooner. When automation tools are layered into operations through platforms that support <a href="https://www.salesforce.com/artificial-intelligence/embedded-ai/" target="_self">embedded AI</a>, those decisions can happen directly inside existing workflows instead of through separate systems.</p>

  1271→

  1321→    
  1322→    <div class="container container--min-spacing-bottom container--min-spacing-top">
  1323→        <article class="grid grid_main text__blade--link_below text--left
  1324→        "
  1325→                 data-module-id="1777668313948_19mo" data-module-name="Risk mitigation, resilience &amp; digital twins" data-module-position="1" data-module-type="headline" data-module-track-impressions="">
  1326→            
  1327→<header class="textmainbody__blade-heading grid grid_sub">
  1328→    
  1329→        
  1330→            
  1331→
  1332→<h2 class="headline text__headline--large" style=" ">
  1333→    
  1334→        Risk mitigation, resilience & digital twins
  1335→    
  1336→
  1337→</h2>
  1338→
  1339→        
  1340→    
  1341→</header>
  1342→    
  1343→    <div class="blade__description">
  1344→        
  1345→    <p>Risk in supply chains rarely arrives as a single event. A weather disruption delays a shipment, or a supplier experiences labor issues. When several disruptions overlap, inventory can run short and production schedules begin to slip. Tariffs shift, fuel costs fluctuate, and the pressure spreads quickly across the network.</p>
  1346→<p>AI supply chain systems surface early warning signs by tracking performance patterns across the network. When a supplier begins trending behind schedule, that shift becomes visible before inventory runs short. If a shipping lane starts showing repeated delays, planners can review alternate paths while there is still flexibility to adjust.</p>
  1347→<p>Process intelligence is what connects those signals to action. By mapping how work actually moves across systems, teams can see where delays are forming, how decisions are made, and what needs to change in response. This especially matters in supply chains where workflows vary across regions, partners, and systems.</p>
  1348→<p>A digital twin supports this work. It’s a living model of the supply chain that reflects how materials and capacity flow through the network. Teams can test “what if” scenarios inside that model and see how a disruption would affect fulfillment or cost before changing anything in production.</p>
  1349→
  1350→
  1351→    </div>
  1352→    
  1353→
  1354→        </article>
  1355→    </div>

  1425→<p>That insight makes contingency planning more concrete. Response options can be evaluated ahead of time, with a clearer view of the tradeoffs involved.</p>

  1502→<p>AI helps connect those signals so leaders can see how the network is performing as a whole. No need for multiple resorts. Planners can monitor fulfillment progress, supplier performance, and transportation flow in one place. When delays begin forming or demand shifts, the change becomes visible earlier.</p>
  1503→<p>That visibility supports faster decisions. Teams can adjust production plans, redirect inventory, or update transportation capacity while there is still room to respond.</p>

  1655→        
  1656→    <p>Supply chain operations involve thousands of small decisions every day. Across your different departments, people are deciding which orders need to be prioritized or if there are shipment delays that need to be communicated. Much of this work still depends on people monitoring dashboards and responding when something changes.</p>
  1657→<p>AI agents help manage that workload. These systems monitor operational signals and carry out defined tasks when certain conditions appear. If inventory drops below a threshold, the agent can flag the issue and prepare a replenishment recommendation. If a shipment falls behind schedule, it can alert the planning team and surface alternate options.</p>
  1658→<p>This kind of support from the <a href="https://www.salesforce.com/agentforce/ai-agents/best-ai-agents/" target="_self">best AI agents</a> reduces the time teams spend tracking routine issues and helps them focus on higher-impact decisions.</p>

  1800→        AI supply chain news and industry momentum
  1801→    
  1802→
  1803→</h2>

  1806→    
  1807→</header>
  1808→    
  1809→    <div class="blade__description">
  1810→        
  1811→    <p>Uncertain conditions will never go away, which is why organizations are investing more in supply chain AI to better respond to that uncertainty and stabilize their operations. Recent supply chain AI news highlights how companies are applying artificial intelligence to forecasting, logistics coordination, and network planning to reduce disruption and improve responsiveness.</p>
  1812→<p>Large manufacturers and retailers are expanding their use of predictive analytics and digital twin models to better understand how their supply networks behave under stress. Technology providers are also embedding AI capabilities directly into planning and logistics platforms, making these tools easier to adopt without large system overhauls.</p>
  1813→<p>Government initiatives are evolving alongside these changes, too. Port modernization programs, digital customs systems, and new traceability requirements are pushing supply chains toward more transparent and data-driven operations.</p>

  1868→        "
  1869→                 data-module-id="1777672306890_g8w" data-module-name="How to implement AI in supply chain operations" data-module-position="1" data-module-type="headline" data-module-track-impressions="">
  1870→            
  1871→<header class="textmainbody__blade-heading grid grid_sub">
  1872→    
  1873→        
  1874→            
  1875→
  1876→<h2 class="headline text__headline--large" style=" ">
  1877→    
  1878→        How to implement AI in supply chain operations
  1879→    
  1880→
  1881→</h2>
  1882→
  1883→        
  1884→    
  1885→</header>
  1886→    
  1887→    <div class="blade__description">
  1888→        
  1889→    <p>Most organizations don’t roll out supply chain AI all at once. The work usually begins by identifying where better forecasting, routing, or inventory decisions would have the most operational impact.</p>
  1890→<p>Data readiness is the first consideration. AI models rely on accurate signals from systems such as ERP platforms, warehouse software, and transportation tools. When those data sources are accessible and reasonably clean, predictive models become much easier to deploy.</p>
  1891→<p>From there, many teams start with a focused pilot. Demand forecasting and logistics planning are common entry points because they influence both cost and service performance. Once models begin producing reliable insights, companies can expand AI capabilities into additional areas of the network.</p>
  1892→<p>AI agents can also be introduced gradually. Early deployments often focus on monitoring operational signals and surfacing exceptions. As confidence in these agents grows, they can take on more coordination tasks across planning, logistics, and inventory workflows.</p>
  1893→<p>Adoption usually expands as teams gain confidence in the models. A forecasting pilot might begin with a single product line or region. Once the results prove reliable, the same approach can be applied to additional categories, warehouses, or distribution networks. Over time, AI becomes part of routine planning rather than a separate project run by the data team.</p>
  1894→

  1967→    <div class="blade__description">
  1968→        
  1969→    <p>Many organizations struggle with supply chain visibility because operational data sits across multiple systems. But with Salesforce, there’s no reason to keep your planning tools, CRM, logistics platform, and your inventory software separate. You can have all of these resources and data points in a single, unified platform.</p>
  1970→<p>When operational data is connected with the <a href="https://www.salesforce.com/artificial-intelligence/business/best-ai-tools-for-business/" target="_self">best AI tools for business</a>, AI can analyze patterns across forecasting, fulfillment, and transportation workflows without requiring teams to manually combine reports from different systems.</p>
  1971→<p>Agentforce builds on that foundation by introducing AI agents that monitor operational activity and coordinate tasks across systems. These agents track shipment updates, surface emerging inventory issues, and escalate exceptions when human review is required. With AI capabilities embedded directly into business applications, planners and operations leaders can access predictive insights while they are already working in their existing workflows.</p>
  1972→<p>For teams exploring how these capabilities fit into their operations, a live <a href="https://www.salesforce.com/agentforce/demos/overview/?d=sh" target="_self">Agentforce demo</a> shows how AI agents can support supply chain workflows in practice.</p>
